**"And That Is a Day That Shall Be Witnessed" — The Word That the Sūrah Closes With**

The *final* *clause* states the *word* that the *sūrah* gives with *particular* *weight*: *wa-dhālika yawmun shahīd* — "and that is a Day that shall be witnessed." The *shahīd* — the *witnessed* — is the *word* that the *commentators* *note* is *the Day on which all human beings, from the beginning of creation to its end, will be present and stand before God when He calls them to account for their actions during their time on earth*, and that the *witnessing* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Day* is *not a* *Day that the wrongdoers can approach without the witness*, in the *sense of being* *the Day that the sūrah characterizes as the witnessed*, in the *sense of being* *the Day that the account holds in the witnessing. The sūrah is stating, in the word, that the *Makkans* are *hearing the statement of the witnessed* *applied to their own Day*, and that the *applied to their own Day* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *Day* is *the witnessed*, in the way described in *verse 18's statement of the witnesses who say: these are the ones who lied against their Lord. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *closing*, that the *next verse* — the *delay of the Day* — is *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *witnessed is not the end of the statement of the Day*, in the way described in *verse 104.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:104

> **And We delay it not, save for a limited term.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"And We Delay It Not" — The Sūrah's Statement of the Day's Delay**

The sūrah now states the *delay* of the *Day* in a *single* *clause*: *wa-mā nunhī-hū illā li-ajalin maʿdūd* — "and We delay it not, save for a limited term." The *statement* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Day* is *not a* *Day that the sūrah holds in the state of the not coming*, in the *sense of being* *the Day that the sūrah delays in the state of the coming*, in the *sense of being* *the Day that the muddat of verse 8 holds in the delay. The sūrah is stating, in the statement, that the *Makkans'* *question* — the *question of verse 8* about *what holds the punishment back* — is *not* *a question that the sūrah answers by the delay*, in the way described in *verse 8's statement of the muddat. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *Makkans* are *hearing the statement of the delay* *applied to their own Day*, and that the *hearing the statement* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *Day* is *delayed in the state of the coming*, in the way described in *verse 39's statement of the punishment that is near.

**"Save for a Limited Term" — The Word That the Sūrah Gives**

The *final* *clause* states the *word* that the *sūrah* gives with *particular* *weight*: *ilā ajalin maʿdūd* — "save for a limited term." The *ajal maʿdūd* — the *limited term*, the *appointed term* — is the *word* that the *sūrah* *used* in *verse 3* for the *term of the goodly enjoyment*, and the *recurrence* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *term of the Day* is *the same kind of term* as the *term of the goodly enjoyment*, in the *sense of being* *the term that the sūrah holds in the state of the appointed*, in the *sense of being* *the term that the commenters note refers to the fact that the time of the Day of Judgment is already known to God*. The sūrah is stating, in the recurrence, that the *Makkans* are *hearing the statement of the limited term* *applied to their own Day*, and that the *applied to their own Day* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *Day* is *within the limited term*, in the way described in *verse 3's statement of the appointed term. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *Makkans'* *delay* is *not a* *delay that the Makkans can extend by their refusal*; it is *the delay that the sūrah holds in the state of the limited term*, in the way described in *verse 8's statement of the muddat that the mocked will beset. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *closing*, that the *next verse* — the *Day on which no soul shall speak save by His Leave* — is *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *limited term is not the end of the statement of the Day*, in the way described in *verse 105.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:105

> **On the Day it comes, no soul shall speak save by His Leave. Among them shall be the wretched and the felicitous.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Day Arrives — and the First Thing Taken Away Is Speech**

The sūrah has spent more than a hundred verses narrating the fate of community after community, and now it stops narrating and states the Day itself. The verse opens with *yawma yaʾti* — "on the Day it comes" — and the pronoun has no stated antecedent, which the commentators take as deliberate: the Day is by this point so established in the reader's mind that it needs no noun.

What the verse then does is unexpected. It does not describe fire, or reckoning, or the splitting of the sky. It describes silence. *Lā takallamu nafsun illā bi-idhnihī* — "no soul shall speak save by His Leave." The first feature of the Last Day, in this sūrah's telling, is that ordinary speech is withdrawn.

The commentators connect this to a set of parallel passages, and the connections sharpen the point. In 78:38 the scene is given in full: *That Day the Spirit and the angels stand in rows, none speaking, save one whom the Compassionate permits, and who speaks aright.* In 20:108 the auditory detail is supplied: *And the voices will be humbled before the Compassionate, and you will hear naught but a murmur.* And in 36:65 the reason emerges — the mouths are sealed and the hands and feet bear witness instead.

Taken together, these passages describe not an absence of sound but a transfer of authority. Speech on that Day is not suppressed so much as re-licensed. What can be said is determined by permission, and the permission is granted to those who *speak aright*.

**"Save by His Leave" — Permission as the Condition of All Speech**

The clause *illā bi-idhnihī* is the hinge of the verse, and it reverses the ordinary human condition. In this world speech is the one capacity nobody can fully take from a person; a prisoner may be silenced by force, but the capacity remains, and inner speech cannot be reached at all. The verse describes a Day in which even that is held on licence.

The commentators note that the restriction is not arbitrary but structural. On that Day speech has consequences that are finally visible, so speech is finally regulated. The murmur of 20:108 is what remains when the unlicensed voice is removed.

There is a pastoral edge here that the sūrah's earlier verses prepare. Throughout Hūd the disbelievers are shown speaking — challenging, mocking, demanding that the punishment be hastened. The verse answers them at the level of the faculty they have been misusing. The capacity to speak is not abolished; it is shown to have been borrowed.

The phrase *bi-idhnihī* also does quiet theological work. Permission implies a permitted thing, and the commentators read 78:38's *wa-ṣāba* — "and who speaks aright" — as indicating that the permission is given precisely so that truth may finally be said without obstruction. The silence is not the point; unobstructed truthfulness is.

**Two Groups, Named Before They Are Described**

The second sentence of the verse is abrupt: *fa-minhum shaqīyun wa-saʿīd* — "among them shall be the wretched and the felicitous." There is no verb of division, no explanation of how the sorting happens. The verse simply names the two outcomes and leaves the following two verses to describe them.

The commentators explain that *al-ashqiyāʾ* and *al-suʿadāʾ* — the wretched and the felicitous — are among the more commonly used terms in Islamic texts for the damned and the saved respectively. The pairing is not this sūrah's invention; it belongs to the shared vocabulary of the tradition.

What is notable is the placement. The division is announced before either group is described, and before any criterion is given. The reader is told the shape of the outcome — two groups, not a spectrum — and then, in vv. 106–108, shown what each group's existence is like.

The effect is to make the descriptions that follow feel like elaboration rather than argument. The sūrah is not persuading the reader that a division exists; it treats the division as settled and turns to its texture.

**The Wretched and the Felicitous in the Later Tradition**

The commentators remark that much has been written about these two terms, especially in Sufi literature, and the remark is worth pausing on because it marks the verse as a point of departure rather than a conclusion.

In the later tradition the pair became technical. The *shaqī* and the *saʿīd* were discussed as fixed categories, and the question of whether one could pass from one to the other generated substantial argument. The verse itself says nothing about movement between the groups; it states their existence at the Day.

This is a place where the exegetical tradition extends well beyond the text, and the distinction matters. The Qur'anic verse describes an outcome; the later literature turned the outcome into a metaphysics. Reading the verse requires holding those apart.

The sūrah's own usage is narrower and more concrete. *Shaqāwa* in Hūd is consistently tied to what a community did — the rejection of messengers, the persistence in corruption — and *saʿāda* to what it accepted. The verse names the two ends of that pattern without abstracting it into a doctrine of fixed destinies.

**Why the Silence Comes First**

There is a structural reason for the order of this verse that the sūrah's design supports. Verse 104 has just spoken of the Day that gathers mankind, and verse 105 begins by removing the faculty that has dominated every preceding narrative. Throughout Hūd the drama has been verbal: prophets speak, peoples answer, arguments are exchanged, threats are issued and mocked.

The Day described here is the end of that drama, and the verse marks it by ending the medium. The dialogues that fill the sūrah's first hundred verses cannot continue into this scene, because speech itself now requires permission.

This also explains why the two groups are named so flatly. With speech withdrawn, there is no courtroom rhetoric, no final plea, no eloquence that might improve a case. The verse removes the possibility that the outcome could be affected by how well a person argues — which is precisely the possibility the disbelievers in the earlier narratives kept assuming.

The reader is left with a scene stripped to its essentials: a Day, a silence, and two named groups. The following verses supply the rest.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:106

> **As for those who are wretched, they shall be in the Fire, wherein there shall be for them groaning and wailing,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The First Group Described — and Described by Sound**

The verse takes up the first of the two groups named in v. 105 and begins its description. The structure is deliberate: after a verse about silence, this one opens with noise. *Fa-amma lladhīna shaqū fa-fī n-nāri lahum fīhā zafīrun wa-shahīq* — "as for those who are wretched, they shall be in the Fire, wherein there shall be for them groaning and wailing."

The commentators distinguish the two terms. *Zafīr* is the sound of the first exhalation, the heavy drawn breath of distress; *shahīq* is the sob that follows, the intake of breath after weeping. The pairing describes not a single cry but the rhythm of unending distress — the breath out and the breath in, repeated without relief.

The choice is significant after v. 105. Speech has been withdrawn, and what replaces it is involuntary sound. The wretched are not described as arguing, protesting, or explaining. They are described as breathing in pain. The faculty that dominated the sūrah's narratives has been reduced to its physiological remainder.

The commentators also note the doubled preposition in *lahum fīhā* — "therein, for them." The construction emphasizes containment: the sound is not emitted toward anyone. There is no audience on that Day, no one to address, and the groaning has no addressee.

**"As for Those Who Were Wretched" — the Perfect Tense**

The relative clause uses the past tense: *alladhīna shaqū*, "those who were wretched," not "those who are wretched." The commentators read this as indicating that the wretchedness is settled before the Day arrives. The Day does not make them wretched; it discloses a condition already fixed.

This connects the verse back to the sūrah's central pattern. Every narrative in Hūd has shown a community given warning, given time, and then choosing. The wretchedness named here is the sum of those choices, and the perfect tense records it as completed.

The verse is careful, however, about where the causation lies. It does not say that God made them wretched; it says that they were wretched, and that they are now in the Fire. The sūrah's consistent pattern is that the messenger offers and the people refuse, and this verse is the final entry in that ledger.

The commentators link the verse to the immediately preceding silence. Because speech requires permission, no one in this group can renegotiate. The perfect tense and the withdrawn speech work together: what is done is done, and there is no forum in which to reopen it.

**Fire as Location, Not Event**

The verse describes the Fire as a place the wretched are *in* — *fa-fī n-nāri* — rather than as something that happens to them. The preposition matters. This is not a punishment administered and concluded; it is a dwelling.

The following verse confirms the reading, speaking of abiding therein for as long as the heavens and the earth endure. The Fire in this passage is a state of existence, and the groaning and wailing are its ambient sound.

The commentators note that the sūrah's Fire-scenes consistently work this way. The punishment is described in terms of duration and location rather than intensity, which shifts the reader's attention from the pain to the permanence.

There is a rhetorical reason for the choice. Intensity can be endured if it ends; permanence is what makes the prospect unendurable. By describing location and duration, the verse makes the warning about loss rather than about pain.

**The Groaning as the End of Argument**

Throughout Hūd the disbelievers are articulate. They demand signs, they question the prophet's humanity, they ask why he has no treasure or garden, they challenge him to bring the punishment he threatens. Their speech is confident and often clever.

The verse answers all of it with two nouns for involuntary breathing. The commentators see the contrast as intentional: the eloquence that filled the earlier narratives resolves into *zafīr* and *shahīq*, sounds that carry no argument and can persuade no one.

This is the sūrah's answer to the objection that the disbelievers might have had a case. Whatever they said, the verse implies, it amounted to this in the end. The reduction of speech to sobbing is not a punishment added to the Fire; it is the natural terminus of speech that was never going to be heard.

The doubled preposition reinforces it. *Lahum fīhā* — the sound belongs to them and stays within it. There is no one to hear, and therefore nothing to say.

**What the Verse Leaves Unsaid**

The verse is remarkably restrained about the Fire. It names no torturers, no chains, no boiling water — all of which appear elsewhere in the Qur'an. It gives a location and two sounds.

The commentators note that this restraint is characteristic of Hūd's closing section, which is concerned less with the apparatus of punishment than with its finality. The sūrah has already made its case across a dozen narratives; it does not need to accumulate horrors.

The restraint also keeps the focus where v. 105 put it: on speech and its withdrawal. The only detail supplied is auditory, and the sound supplied is not language. The verse completes the thought begun in the previous one by showing what remains when permission to speak is not granted.

The reader is meant to carry the sound into v. 107, where the duration is given. The groaning is not a moment; it is the atmosphere of an abiding.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:107

> **abiding therein for so long as the heavens and the earth endure, save as thy Lord wills. Surely thy Lord does whatsoever He wills.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Duration Measured Against the Cosmos**

The verse gives the duration of what v. 106 described, and it does so by an unusual measure. *Khālidīna fīhā mā dāmati s-samāwātu wa-l-arḍ* — "abiding therein for so long as the heavens and the earth endure." The commentators note that this is an Arabic idiom for permanence, drawing on the pre-Islamic habit of describing anything lasting as lasting like the heavens and the earth.

The idiom would have been immediately intelligible to the first hearers as meaning "for ever." But the commentators also observe that the Qur'an chooses a measure that is itself created. The heavens and the earth are not eternal in Islamic teaching; they are made and will pass. Tying the duration to them is therefore a way of expressing permanence in human terms without asserting that the punishment is co-eternal with God.

The phrase does real work in the sūrah's argument. The disbelievers in the earlier narratives repeatedly treat the threat as rhetorical — something a prophet says to be persuasive. The verse answers by giving the threat a timescale, and by giving it the largest timescale available to the imagination.

The commentators connect this to the sūrah's repeated emphasis on respite. Every community in Hūd was given time and used it. The verse describes the end of respite in the only terms that make its absence comprehensible.

**"Save as Thy Lord Wills" — the Exception and Its Function**

The clause *illā mā shāʾa rabbuk* — "save as thy Lord wills" — has generated extensive discussion, and the commentators preserve the range of readings rather than settling it.

One reading takes the exception as applying to the period before entry: the wretched abide in the Fire for as long as the heavens and the earth endure, except for the time their Lord wills otherwise, which may be the interval of the reckoning or the time spent elsewhere. On this reading the exception modifies the commencement, not the duration.

A second reading takes it as a statement of divine freedom — the abiding is as stated, but it remains subject to God's will, which is not bound by the statement. This reading treats the exception as guarding against the conclusion that anything, even a declared punishment, constrains God.

The commentators note that the same clause appears in v. 108 with respect to the felicitous, followed there by the words "a gift unfailing." The asymmetry is significant: for the blessed the verse explicitly removes the uncertainty, while for the wretched it is left standing. The exegetical tradition has generally read this as indicating that the promise of reward is unqualified while the threat retains the possibility of mercy.

It is worth recording that this asymmetry is a matter of interpretation rather than of explicit statement. The verse does not say that the Fire will end; it says that the abiding is subject to God's will. The tradition's confidence that mercy remains possible rests on that openness, not on a promise.

**"Surely Thy Lord Does Whatsoever He Wills"**

The verse closes with *inna rabbaka faʿʿālun li-mā yurīd* — "surely thy Lord does whatsoever He wills." The commentators read this as the resolution of the preceding exception. Having introduced the possibility that God's will modifies the stated duration, the verse affirms that God's will is effective.

The intensive form *faʿʿāl* is worth noting. It is not merely "does" but "is ever-doing" — the commentators take it as indicating that God's willing is not occasional but constant, and that what He wills is done without intermediary or obstruction.

In the sūrah's argument this closes a thread begun much earlier. The disbelievers have repeatedly doubted that the threatened punishment would come, on the grounds that it kept not coming. The verse answers that the delay is not incapacity. The one who delays is the one who acts, and He acts when He wills.

The commentators also connect the phrase to the sūrah's treatment of divine patience. Hūd repeatedly shows God giving communities time — Noah's centuries, Hūd's years, the respite granted to every people. The verse frames that patience as an exercise of will rather than an absence of power.

**Heavens and Earth as a Measure the Reader Can Hold**

The choice of measure deserves a further word. The Qur'an could have said "for ever" directly, and elsewhere it uses phrases of unending duration. Here it ties the punishment to something the reader can see.

The commentators note that the heavens and the earth were, for the first hearers, the paradigm of durability. Everything else in human experience decayed; those did not. Anchoring the duration to them made the abstract concrete.

There is also a polemical edge. The sūrah's disbelievers are attached to this world — its gardens, its treasures, its status. The verse measures the punishment by the very things they value, and in doing so suggests that the world's own permanence is the wrong standard. The heavens and the earth will not outlast the Fire; on the Qur'an's teaching they will not outlast the Day.

The measure is thus both comprehensible and subversive. It gives the reader something to hold while quietly undermining the assumption that the created order is the fixed background against which everything else is measured.

**The Verse in the Sūrah's Arc**

Verse 107 completes the description of the wretched begun in v. 106, and it does so by adding only two things: duration and divine freedom. No new torment is introduced, no further detail of the Fire.

This economy is characteristic of the closing section. The sūrah has made its case narratively, and the closing verses summarize rather than elaborate. The commentators note that the shift from story to statement begins around v. 100 and continues to the end.

The verse also prepares the transition to v. 108. Having described the wretched with a duration qualified by divine will, the sūrah turns to the felicitous with the same duration and a different qualification. The reader is meant to notice the parallel and then the difference.

That difference — "a gift unfailing" — is where the sūrah's pastoral purpose becomes explicit. The threat is stated with an opening left in it; the promise is closed.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:108

> **And as for those who are felicitous, they shall be in the Garden, abiding therein for so long as the heavens and the earth endure, save as thy Lord wills—a gift unfailing.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Group — and the Parallel Deliberately Kept**

The verse takes up the second group named in v. 105, and its structure mirrors v. 107 almost exactly. *Wa-amma lladhīna suʿidū fa-fī l-jannati khālidīna fīhā mā dāmati s-samāwātu wa-l-arḍ* — "and as for those who are felicitous, they shall be in the Garden, abiding therein for so long as the heavens and the earth endure." The same measure, the same exception, the same permanence.

The commentators note that the mirroring is intentional. The two outcomes are given in the same terms so that the reader compares them directly, and so that the single difference between them stands out.

That difference is the final phrase: *ʿaṭāʾan ghayra majdhūdh* — "a gift unfailing." It has no counterpart in v. 107. The wretched receive a duration qualified by divine will and nothing more; the felicitous receive the same duration plus an explicit guarantee.

The commentators read the asymmetry as the verse's central point. Both abidings are subject to God's will in the abstract, but the sūrah chooses to reassure about one and not the other. The promise is closed; the threat is left open.

**"A Gift" — the Word That Changes the Category**

The noun *ʿaṭāʾ* is significant. It denotes a gift, a grant, something given rather than earned. The commentators emphasize that the Garden is described here not as a wage but as a bestowal.

This matters because the sūrah has spent its closing verses emphasizing deeds. Verse 111 will say that God pays each in full for what they did, and the language of payment is wage-language. Verse 108 uses different language for the same destination.

The commentators reconcile the two by noting that the deed is the condition, not the price. What is given exceeds what is done, and the word *ʿaṭāʾ* marks the excess.

The adjective *ghayra majdhūdh* — "unfailing," literally "not cut off" — completes the thought. The root *j-dh-dh* carries the sense of severing, and the commentators gloss the phrase as a gift that is not interrupted, diminished, or withdrawn. Whatever is given continues.

**The Same Measure, the Different Guarantee**

The commentators have long noted the exegetical consequence of the parallel. Because v. 107 and v. 108 share the phrase "save as thy Lord wills," the exception cannot function differently in the two cases by grammar alone. What differs is what the sūrah adds.

On the threat side, the exception stands bare. The tradition has generally read this as preserving the possibility that God's mercy may exceed His stated punishment — a possibility asserted nowhere explicitly but left unexcluded.

On the promise side, the exception is immediately followed by "a gift unfailing," which the commentators read as removing the uncertainty. The gift will not be cut off, so the exception does not introduce doubt about it.

It should be recorded that this reading is an inference rather than a statement. The verse does not say that the Fire will end or that the Garden cannot be lost. It says that one is a gift that does not fail. The exegetical confidence about the final fate of the wretched rests on the openness of v. 107, not on any promise in it.

**Garden as Dwelling, Answering Fire as Dwelling**

Verse 106 described the wretched as *in* the Fire, and v. 108 describes the felicitous as *in* the Garden. The commentators note the symmetry of location. Neither is described as experiencing something; both are described as inhabiting somewhere.

This is consistent with the sūrah's closing method. Having exhausted narrative, it describes the two outcomes as states of being rather than as events. The Fire has its sound — groaning and wailing; the Garden has its character — a gift that does not fail.

The contrast in what is supplied is itself meaningful. For the wretched the sūrah supplies sensory detail; for the felicitous it supplies a legal guarantee. The commentators read this as addressing two different anxieties: the fear of pain, and the fear of loss.

The second is the more sophisticated of the two, and the verse treats it as the more serious. The Garden is not described as painless but as unending. What is guaranteed is not the quality of the experience but its permanence.

**Why the Sūrah Ends Its Eschatology Here**

Verses 105–108 form a complete unit: the Day and its silence, the two groups, and the two abidings. After v. 108 the sūrah turns to instruction — steadfastness, prayer, patience — and does not return to description of the hereafter in this register.

The commentators note that the placement is deliberate. The eschatological summary comes after the narratives and before the commands, so that the commands are given against a settled background. The reader is not asked to act in uncertainty; the outcomes have been stated.

The verse's closing phrase does the final work. "A gift unfailing" is the last word on the hereafter in this section, and it is a word of assurance rather than of warning. The sūrah has made its threat and then chosen to end the unit on the promise.

The commands that follow in vv. 112–115 are therefore not presented as the means of escaping a stated doom but as the response of those who have been given a promise. The shift in tone from v. 106 to v. 108 is what makes that framing possible.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:109

> **So be not in doubt concerning that which these [people] worship; they worship only as their fathers worshipped before. We shall surely give them their share, undiminished.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Instruction Not to Doubt — Addressed to Whom?**

The verse opens with a prohibition of doubt: *fa-lā taku fī miryatin mimmā yaʿbudu hāʾulāʾ* — "so be not in doubt concerning that which these worship." The singular address has led the commentators to ask who is being spoken to, and the answers differ.

Al-Bayḍāwī reads the verse as directed to the Prophet, and takes its content to be that there should be no doubt concerning the final outcome for the Makkan idolaters. On this reading the prohibition is not about the fact of their worship — which is visible — but about its consequence. The Prophet is told not to hesitate over whether the pattern established in the preceding narratives will apply to them.

Others read the address as general, in which case the verse speaks to any hearer who might wonder whether the Makkans are an exception to the sūrah's rule. The sūrah has just described a dozen destroyed communities; the verse forecloses the suggestion that this one is different.

Both readings agree on the substance. The doubt being prohibited is not about what the Makkans worship but about whether it will matter. The verse answers that it will, and that the reason is already visible in what has been narrated.

**"As Their Fathers Worshipped Before" — Inheritance as the Diagnosis**

The middle clause gives the reason for the certainty: *mā yaʿbudūna illā kamā yaʿbudu ābāʾuhum min qabl* — "they worship only as their fathers worshipped before." The commentators note that the *illā kamā* construction is restrictive. Their worship is not merely similar to their fathers'; it is nothing other than that.

This is the sūrah's diagnosis of idolatry, and it is not primarily theological. The verse does not say the Makkans are wrong about the nature of God or confused about creation. It says their religion is inherited and unexamined.

The commentators connect the verse to v. 62, where the Thamūd are described in the same terms — given to the ways of their fathers. The parallel is what makes the diagnosis a pattern rather than an insult. Every community in the sūrah is shown doing the same thing.

The word *min qabl* — "before" — adds the temporal dimension. The worship is not chosen in the present; it arrives from the past and is passed on unchanged. The verse identifies continuity as the problem, which is a striking thing for a text that elsewhere honors lineage and tradition.

The commentators are careful here. The objection is not to having fathers or to receiving a tradition; the Prophet's own message is presented as continuous with Abraham's. The objection is to a tradition held without examination, where the only reason given for a practice is that it was practiced before.

**"Their Share, Undiminished" — the Word the Commentators Explain**

The verse closes with *wa-innā la-muwaffūhum naṣībahum ghayra manqūṣ* — "and We shall surely give them their share, undiminished." The word *naṣīb* ordinarily means a portion, and often a good one; the commentators therefore note that its sense here is fixed by context.

Ibn Kathīr explains that the share the Makkan idolaters will receive is punishment. The phrase is thus an ironic use of the language of allotment: they will receive their portion in full, and the portion is what they have earned.

The commentators connect this to 11:15–16, where the same logic appears — those who desire the life of this world are paid their works therein without diminution, and in the hereafter they have nothing but the Fire. The parallel establishes that *naṣīb* in this register means what is due, not what is desirable.

The adjective *ghayra manqūṣ* — "not diminished" — is emphatic. Nothing will be subtracted from what is owed. The commentators read this as answering an unspoken hope that the punishment might be reduced, whether through intercession, ancestral merit, or divine reluctance.

**Why the Verse Comes Here**

The placement of the verse matters. It follows immediately upon the eschatological unit of vv. 105–108, which described the two abidings and closed on the assurance of "a gift unfailing." The verse now turns back to the present audience.

The commentators see this as the pivot of the sūrah's closing movement. The narratives are finished, the outcomes are stated, and the verse applies both to the people actually listening. The Makkans are not a future case or a distant analogy; they are *hāʾulāʾ* — "these," the ones nearby.

The shift from narrative to direct address is what gives the verse its force. For a hundred verses the sūrah has spoken about destroyed peoples in the third person. Here it uses the demonstrative of proximity. The reader is told that the pattern just described is not history but diagnosis.

The prohibition of doubt fits this pivot. Having seen the pattern repeated a dozen times, hesitation would be unwarranted. The verse treats the conclusion as already reached by the evidence the sūrah has supplied.

**The Verse and the Sūrah's Argument About Time**

There is a final thread worth drawing. Throughout Hūd the disbelievers' chief objection is temporal: the punishment was promised and has not come. Noah's people mocked him across centuries; Hūd's people demanded the threatened wind; the Makkans ask when the promise will be fulfilled if he is truthful.

The verse answers at the level of certainty rather than timing. It does not say when; it says that the share will be given in full and not diminished. The delay that the disbelievers treat as evidence against the threat is treated here as irrelevant to it.

This connects to the phrase *ghayra manqūṣ*. A portion that is not diminished is a portion that has been kept. The commentators read the emphasis as indicating that the delay does not erode what is owed; it accumulates.

The sūrah's closing verses return to this repeatedly. Verse 115 will speak of patience and of God not neglecting the reward of the virtuous; the same logic runs in reverse. Neither the reward nor the share is lost through the passage of time.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:110

> **Indeed, We gave unto Moses the Book; then difference arose regarding it. And were it not for a Word that had preceded from thy Lord, judgment would have been rendered between them. Yet, truly they are confounded by doubt regarding it.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Moses Introduced — and the Point Is Not Moses**

The verse turns abruptly to Moses: *wa-laqad ātaynā Mūsā l-kitāba fa-kh-tulifa fīh* — "indeed, We gave unto Moses the Book; then difference arose regarding it." The commentators note that the verse is not primarily about Moses. Moses appears as the precedent for what is happening to the Prophet.

The structure is compressed. Two facts are stated in one breath: the Book was given, and difference arose about it. There is no narrative, no account of what the difference was. The verse assumes the hearer knows the story and uses it as a pattern.

The commentators read the conjunction *fa-* — "then" — as significant. Difference did not merely accompany the giving; it followed from it. The Book itself became the occasion of division, which is a claim about revelation rather than about its recipients.

In the sūrah's argument this does specific work. The Prophet's opponents have treated disagreement about his message as evidence against it. The verse answers that disagreement followed the clearest revelation the tradition remembers. Difference is not a diagnostic of falsehood.

**"A Word That Had Preceded from Thy Lord"**

The second clause is the verse's hinge: *wa-law lā kalimatun sabaqat min rabbika la-quḍiya baynahum* — "and were it not for a Word that had preceded from thy Lord, judgment would have been rendered between them."

The commentators explain that the *kalima* is the divine decree postponing judgment to the Day. Had that word not preceded, the disagreement would have been settled immediately and decisively — the truth would have been made manifest and the disagreement ended by force of evidence.

The conditional is counterfactual. Judgment *would* have been rendered, and the reason it has not been is a prior commitment. The commentators read this as answering the disbelievers' demand for immediate resolution: the delay is not indecision but fidelity to a stated intention.

This connects to the sūrah's treatment of respite throughout. Every community is given time, and the giving of time is consistently described as deliberate. The verse frames the entire historical interval — from Moses to Muhammad — as governed by the same postponement.

The phrase *min rabbika* — "from thy Lord" — is addressed to the Prophet, and the commentators note the reassurance in it. The postponement is not abandonment. The one who delayed is the one who will judge.

**"Confounded by Doubt" — the State the Verse Names**

The verse closes with *wa-innahum la-fī shakkin minhu murīb* — "yet truly they are confounded by doubt regarding it." The commentators gloss *murīb* as doubt that causes suspicion and unease, doubt that is not merely absence of conviction but active disturbance.

The word choice is precise. The verse does not say they disbelieve; it says they are in disturbing doubt. The commentators read this as describing a state rather than a position — the doubter is not at rest in unbelief but unsettled by it.

The pronoun in *minhu* refers back to the Book. Their doubt concerns the revelation itself, and the verse describes it as unresolved. They have not settled the question; they have failed to settle it.

This is a sharper claim than simple disbelief, and the commentators note its pastoral edge. A person in *shakk murīb* is not beyond reach. The verse diagnoses the condition of the opponents in a way that leaves the possibility of resolution open.

The connection to v. 109 is worth noting. There the diagnosis was inherited, unexamined practice; here it is unsettled doubt. The two together describe an audience that has neither examined its tradition nor resolved its questions about the new message.

**The Verse as Consolation and as Warning**

The commentators read the verse as doing two things at once. For the Prophet it is consolation: the pattern he is experiencing is not new, and the greatest of the earlier prophets experienced it. For the opponents it is warning: the postponement has an end, and the judgment deferred is not judgment cancelled.

The first function depends on the compressed form. Because the verse gives no detail of the disagreement about Moses, the hearer supplies what they know — and what they know includes that Moses was vindicated. The verse invokes the whole arc without narrating it.

The second function depends on the counterfactual. *La-quḍiya baynahum* states what would have happened but for the prior word. The verse makes the alternative vivid: immediate, decisive judgment. Its absence is a reprieve, and reprieves end.

The sūrah's closing section is built on this double address. The commands that follow — steadfastness in v. 112, patience in v. 115 — are addressed to the Prophet and the believers, while the descriptions of judgment continue to face the opponents. The verse holds both audiences in a single sentence.

**What the Verse Does Not Say**

The verse is careful in what it withholds. It does not name the content of the disagreement about the Book, does not identify who differed, and does not state whether the difference was ever resolved within history.

The commentators note that this reticence is characteristic of the sūrah's closing movement, which summarizes rather than narrates. The detail has already been given for other communities; here the pattern alone is needed.

The withholding also serves the argument. If the verse specified the disagreement, the hearer might treat it as a particular historical dispute with particular causes. Left general, it reads as the structural consequence of revelation to beings who can refuse it.

The final clause reinforces the point. The doubt is described as present and ongoing — *la-fī shakkin* — rather than as a resolved historical matter. The verse keeps the question live for its own audience.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:111

> **Surely for each, thy Lord shall pay them in full for their deeds. Truly He is Aware of what they do.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Payment in Full — the Sūrah's Accounting Language**

The verse states the principle of exact requital: *wa-inna kullan lammā yuwaffiyannahum aʿmālahum* — "surely for each, thy Lord shall pay them in full for their deeds." The verb *waffā* is the language of commercial settlement, of paying what is owed without shortfall.

The commentators note that the same root appeared in v. 109, where the idolaters are promised their *naṣīb* undiminished. The two verses use the vocabulary of payment in opposite registers — there for punishment, here for all deeds — and the shared vocabulary is deliberate.

The emphatic construction *lammā yuwaffiyannahum* carries a doubled assurance. The commentators read the intensification as answering the specific objection that requital might be approximate, that some deeds might go unreckoned or be rounded off.

The word *kull* — "each," "every one" — is comprehensive. The verse does not speak of groups or communities but of individuals. After a hundred verses of collective narrative, the accounting is specified as personal.

**"For Their Deeds" — Not for Their Lineage**

The object of the payment is *aʿmālahum* — their deeds. The commentators connect this directly to the preceding verses, and the contrast is pointed.

Verse 109 diagnosed the idolaters as worshipping as their fathers worshipped. Verse 110 described a community divided over a Book. Verse 111 states that what will be paid for is deeds. The sequence removes the two alternative grounds of standing that the audience might have relied on: ancestry and affiliation.

The commentators note that this is a recurring Qur'anic insistence. Being descended from a prophet or belonging to a community that received revelation is treated throughout as historically significant and soteriologically irrelevant. What settles the account is what a person did.

The verse therefore completes the argument begun at v. 109. The Makkans' inherited worship will not avail them, and neither will their proximity to the Kaʿba or their descent from Abraham. The payment is for deeds, and each person's own.

**"He Is Aware of What They Do" — Knowledge as the Condition of Justice**

The verse closes with *innahu bimā yaʿmalūna khabīr* — "truly He is Aware of what they do." The commentators gloss *khabīr* as one whose knowledge penetrates to the interior of things, not merely one who observes their surface.

The attribute is what makes the promise of exact payment credible. A reckoning that pays each in full requires knowledge of each in full, and the verse supplies it. The commentators read the clause as the ground of the preceding promise rather than an addition to it.

This connects to the sūrah's opening. Verse 5 stated that God knows what they hide and what they disclose, and that He knows what lies within breasts. The closing section returns to the same attribute, now as the basis of judgment rather than as a warning about concealment.

The commentators note the pastoral consequence. If the reckoning is exact and the knowledge is complete, then deeds that went unnoticed by others are not lost. The verse addresses the person whose good was unseen as much as the one whose evil was hidden.

**The Verse Between Two Commands**

Verse 111 sits between the description of the opponents' doubt in v. 110 and the command to steadfastness in v. 112, and its function is transitional.

The commentators read it as supplying the reason the command is possible. Steadfastness under opposition is difficult; the verse assures the one commanded that nothing is being lost in the meantime. The deeds performed while the opponents doubt are being recorded and will be paid.

The verse also answers the disbelievers' implicit objection. They have treated the absence of immediate judgment as evidence that nothing is happening. The verse states that the accounting is continuous and complete, and that its delay is not its absence.

The emphatic *inna* at both the beginning and the end of the verse — *wa-inna kull*, *innahu* — frames the statement with assurance. The commentators note that the doubling is characteristic of the sūrah's closing section, which repeatedly insists where earlier sections simply stated.

**Exactness and Its Limits**

The verse promises exact payment for deeds and does not qualify it. The commentators have noted the tension with the broader Qur'anic teaching that God forgives whom He wills, and they resolve it in various ways.

One reading takes the verse as describing the standard rather than the outcome: what is owed is paid in full, and mercy operates as addition rather than subtraction. On this reading the promise of exactness is a promise of justice, and grace is something further.

Another reading limits the verse's scope to the fact of reckoning rather than its result — every deed will be accounted for, but the accounting does not determine the sentence mechanically.

It is worth recording that the verse itself does not settle the question. It states that payment will be in full and that God is aware of the deeds. What the tradition draws from it about the final balance is inference, and the commentators preserve the range rather than closing it.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:112

> **So be steadfast, as thou hast been commanded—and those who turn in repentance along with you—and be not rebellious. Truly He sees whatsoever you do.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command at the Centre of the Closing Section**

The verse is the sūrah's central command: *fa-staqim kamā umirt* — "so be steadfast, as thou hast been commanded." The commentators note that the imperative *istaqim* carries the sense of holding a straight course, of remaining upright without deviation in either direction.

The qualification *kamā umirt* — "as thou hast been commanded" — is what the commentators emphasize. Steadfastness is not a matter of intensity or of choosing the harder path. It is fidelity to what has already been given. The measure is the command, not the effort.

The verse then extends the command: *wa-man tāba maʿak* — "and those who turn in repentance along with you." The commentators read the conjunction as including the community in the same instruction. Steadfastness is not the Prophet's burden alone.

The phrase *man tāba* — "those who have turned" — identifies the community by repentance rather than by birth or affiliation. The commentators connect this to v. 111's insistence that payment is for deeds. The people addressed here are those who turned, and their standing rests on that.

**"Be Not Rebellious" — the Negative That Defines the Positive**

The verse adds a prohibition: *wa-lā taṭghaw* — "and be not rebellious." The root *ṭ-gh-y* carries the sense of exceeding a limit, of transgressing a bound, and the commentators gloss it here as going beyond what has been commanded.

The pairing is instructive. Steadfastness could be mistaken for excess — for adding to the command out of zeal, for imposing more than was required, for treating rigor as proof of fidelity. The prohibition forecloses that reading.

The commentators note that *ṭughyān* in the Qur'an is consistently the characteristic of the destroyed communities. The sūrah's tyrants are described with this root. The verse therefore warns the believers against the specific failure that destroyed those they have just read about.

This is a sharper instruction than a general warning against sin. It tells the commanded not to exceed the command — which is a failure available only to the obedient. The verse addresses the risk that belongs to those who have already turned.

**"He Sees Whatsoever You Do" — Surveillance as Ground of Steadfastness**

The verse closes with *innahu bimā taʿmalūna baṣīr* — "truly He sees whatsoever you do." The commentators distinguish *baṣīr* from the *khabīr* of v. 111: the first denotes penetrating awareness, the second denotes seeing.

The attribute functions here as support rather than threat. The commentators read it as addressing the loneliness of the command. Steadfastness under opposition often goes unwitnessed; the verse assures the one commanded that it does not go unseen.

The shift in pronoun is worth noting. Verse 111 spoke of what *they* do — the opponents; this verse speaks of what *you* do — the Prophet and those who turned with him. The same divine seeing applies to both, and the verse makes the application to the believers explicit.

The commentators connect this to the sūrah's opening statement of divine knowledge at v. 5. There the knowledge was presented as defeating concealment; here it is presented as sustaining obedience. The attribute is the same; the address differs.

**Why This Command Follows the Narratives**

The placement of the verse is significant. It comes after the destruction of a dozen communities, after the eschatological summary of vv. 105–108, and after the diagnosis of the Makkans at v. 109 and the precedent of Moses at v. 110.

The commentators read the sequence as preparation. The narratives show what happens to communities that refuse; the eschatology shows the two outcomes; the diagnosis shows the present audience in the pattern; the precedent shows that the Prophet's situation is not unprecedented. Only then is the command given.

The command is thus not a general moral instruction but a specific response to a specific predicament. The Prophet has been shown that his opponents are doing what earlier opponents did, and that the outcome is settled. The instruction is to hold the course in the meantime.

The extension to *man tāba maʿak* makes it communal. The believers are told that the same steadfastness is required of them, and that the same prohibition applies. The verse does not reserve the command for the Prophet's rank.

**Steadfastness and the Delay**

There is a thread connecting this verse to the sūrah's persistent theme of delayed judgment. The opponents have mocked the delay throughout; the Prophet has been told at v. 110 that judgment is postponed by a prior word.

The command to steadfastness is what fills the interval. The commentators read it as addressing the difficulty of continuing when the promised vindication has not arrived. Steadfastness is specifically the virtue of the period between promise and fulfillment.

The prohibition of *ṭughyān* connects to the same difficulty. The temptation during a long delay is to exceed — to take matters into one's own hands, to impose by force what has not been granted, to anticipate the judgment that God has postponed.

The verse's closing assurance answers both. He sees what you do. The interval is not unobserved, and what is done in it is being recorded — which is the premise of v. 111's promise of payment in full.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:113

> **And incline not toward the wrongdoers, lest the Fire should touch you—and you will have no protector apart from God. Thereafter you will not be helped.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Prohibition of Inclination — Not of Contact**

The verse addresses the believers with a prohibition: *wa-lā tarkanū ilā lladhīna ẓalamū* — "and incline not toward the wrongdoers." The commentators note that the verb *rakana* denotes leaning, a movement of weight toward something, rather than mere proximity or acquaintance.

The distinction matters. The believers lived among the wrongdoers; they traded with them, were related to them, shared a city with them. The verse does not forbid that. It forbids leaning — the transfer of one's support, one's approval, or one's confidence.

The commentators gloss the inclination variously as approval of their wrongdoing, reliance on them, or affection for their way. The readings converge on a single point: the prohibition concerns the direction of the heart's weight, not the fact of contact.

The verse follows immediately upon the command to steadfastness at v. 112, and the commentators read the sequence as deliberate. Having been told to hold a straight course, the believers are told what would bend it.

**"Lest the Fire Should Touch You" — the Consequence Attached**

The prohibition carries an immediate consequence: *fa-tamassakumu n-nār* — "lest the Fire should touch you." The commentators note the verb *massa*, to touch, which describes contact rather than immersion.

The word choice is precise and the commentators draw attention to it. The verse does not say the Fire will engulf the one who inclines; it says it will touch them. The image is of a consequence that begins at the point of contact and is not confined there.

This reading is supported by the verse's structure. The inclination is described as the act; the touching as its result. There is no intervening judgment scene, no reckoning described. The consequence is presented as following from the act almost physically.

The commentators connect this to the sūrah's broader treatment of the Fire. In vv. 106–107 the Fire was described as a dwelling with a duration; here it is described as something that reaches a person in the course of their life. The two descriptions are not in tension — the touch is the beginning of what the dwelling completes.

**"No Protector Apart from God" — the Isolation Named**

The verse continues: *wa-mā lakum min dūni Llāhi min awliyāʾ* — "and you will have no protector apart from God." The commentators read this as the second half of the consequence, and the more serious half.

The word *awlīyāʾ* is the plural of *walī*, which denotes a protector, an ally, one who stands close and acts on another's behalf. The commentators note that the same root is used in the Qur'an for the relationship between God and the believers, and here its absence is what is announced.

The logic of the verse is that the one who leans on the wrongdoers loses the support they leaned toward and does not gain it. The wrongdoers cannot protect; the verse says so earlier in the sūrah, when the partners the communities called upon vanish at the moment of need. And by inclining toward them, the believer has forfeited the protection that was available.

The commentators emphasize that the phrase *min dūni Llāh* — "apart from God" — is restrictive. It does not say there will be no protectors; it says there will be none besides God. What remains is God alone, and the verse's warning is that the one who inclined has placed themselves in a position where that is all they have — and where they have chosen otherwise.

**"Thereafter You Will Not Be Helped" — the Closing Clause**

The verse ends with *thumma lā tunṣarūn* — "thereafter you will not be helped." The commentators note the particle *thumma*, which indicates sequence with a gap, rather than *fa-*, which indicates immediate succession.

The sequencing is what gives the clause its weight. The touching of the Fire comes first; the absence of help follows. The commentators read this as describing not a single event but a situation that develops — the consequence of inclination is not instantaneous but progressive.

The verb *nuṣira* is the passive of victory and aid. The commentators connect it to the sūrah's promise at various points that the messengers and believers will be helped. The verse states the withdrawal of that help as the consequence of leaning toward the wrongdoers.

There is a pastoral edge in the sequence. The verse does not describe an irreversible state; it describes what follows if the inclination continues. The commentators read the warning as given precisely so that it can be heeded before the sequence completes.

**Why the Verse Addresses the Believers**

It is worth noting who is being spoken to. The verse is not addressed to the wrongdoers but to those who have already turned — the *man tāba maʿak* of v. 112. The prohibition concerns a failure available only to the committed.

The commentators see this as characteristic of the sūrah's closing section, which repeatedly addresses the believers about risks specific to belief. Verse 112 warned against exceeding the command; this verse warns against leaning toward those who transgress it. Both are failures of the obedient, not of the rebellious.

The connection to the narratives is direct. Throughout Hūd the communities are destroyed, and in several cases a small group is saved — Lot's family except his wife, Noah's household except his son. The exceptions are instructive: proximity to a prophet did not save those whose hearts leaned elsewhere.

The verse generalizes the lesson. It is not lineage, association, or residence that determines the outcome but the direction of one's inclination. The believers are told that their standing is not secured by their company but by their weight.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:114

> **And perform the prayer at the two ends of the day and in the early hours of the night. Truly good deeds remove those that are evil. This is a reminder for those who remember.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Prayer at the Two Ends of the Day**

The verse turns from prohibition to positive instruction: *wa-aqimi ṣ-ṣalāta ṭarafayi n-nahār* — "and perform the prayer at the two ends of the day." The commentators identify the two ends as morning and evening, and read the phrase as encompassing the prayers that fall at those times.

The commentators have discussed which prayers are intended, and the readings vary. Some take the two ends as the dawn prayer and the sunset prayer; others include the midday and afternoon prayers within the span. The verse itself does not enumerate, and the commentators preserve the range rather than closing it.

The phrase *wa-zulafan mina l-layl* — "and in the early hours of the night" — adds the night prayers. The word *zulaf* denotes portions or approaches, and the commentators gloss it as the hours near the beginning of the night.

The distribution is significant. The commentators note that the verse frames the day at both its boundaries and extends into the night, so that no extended period passes without prayer. The instruction is about the shape of time rather than about a specific number of acts.

**"Good Deeds Remove Those That Are Evil"**

The verse's central statement is *inna l-ḥasanāti yudhhibna s-sayyiʾāt* — "truly good deeds remove those that are evil." The commentators note the plural on both sides and the generality of the claim.

The verb *adhhaba* denotes removal, taking something away. The commentators distinguish this from forgiveness in the strict sense: the verse describes the good deed as actively displacing the evil, not merely as being weighed against it.

The statement has generated considerable discussion about its scope. Some commentators read it as applying to minor sins, on the grounds that major sins require repentance; others read it generally. The verse does not specify, and the tradition preserves both readings.

It is worth recording that the verse itself makes no distinction. It states that good deeds remove evil deeds, and the qualification some commentators add comes from elsewhere in the tradition rather than from this text. The distinction between minor and major sins is a later systematic development.

The commentators connect the statement to the prayer instruction that precedes it. Prayer is the good deed most regularly available, and the verse's placement suggests that the daily prayers are among the means by which the removal occurs.

**"A Reminder for Those Who Remember"**

The verse closes with *dhālika dhikrā li-dh-dhākirīn* — "this is a reminder for those who remember." The commentators note the cognate construction: the *dhikrā* is for the *dhākirūn*, the reminder for the mindful.

The phrasing is not tautological, and the commentators explain why. The reminder reaches those who are already disposed to remember; it does not create the disposition. The verse identifies its own audience and its own limit.

This connects to the sūrah's recurring theme. Throughout Hūd the messengers remind and the communities refuse, and the refusal is consistently described as a failure of memory rather than of information. The communities are not ignorant; they are unmindful.

The commentators read the closing phrase as both comfort and warning. Comfort, because the reminder is effective for those who receive it; warning, because its effectiveness depends on a disposition the verse does not claim to supply.

**Why Prayer Follows the Prohibition**

The sequence of vv. 112–114 is deliberate, and the commentators note its logic. Verse 112 commands steadfastness and forbids excess; v. 113 forbids inclination toward the wrongdoers and warns of the Fire; v. 114 prescribes prayer and states that good deeds remove evil.

The progression moves from what to avoid to what to do. Having been warned about the direction of the heart's weight, the believer is given a practice that fixes it. The commentators read the prayer as the means by which the prohibition is kept.

The statement about good deeds removing evil addresses a difficulty the preceding verses raise. The believer who has inclined — who has already failed — might conclude that the warning has closed the matter. The verse answers that the good deed removes the evil, and that the practice is available daily.

The distribution of prayer across the day supports this. The commentators note that the removal is not a single act of absolution but a recurring process, matched to the recurring possibility of failure.

**The Verse in the Sūrah's Closing Movement**

Verse 114 is the only verse in the sūrah's closing section that prescribes a specific act of worship. The surrounding verses give commands of disposition — steadfastness, patience, avoidance — but not a practice.

The commentators note that this is characteristic of Hūd, which is largely narrative and hortatory rather than legal. The prayer instruction stands out precisely because the sūrah contains so few comparable prescriptions.

The verse also connects backward to the sūrah's opening. Verse 1 established the Book as made precise and then expounded; v. 114 is an instance of the expounding, taking a general obligation and specifying its times.

The closing phrase returns the verse to the sūrah's central concern. The reminder is for those who remember — which is what the whole sūrah has been attempting to produce. The prayer is given as the practice that sustains it.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:115

> **And be thou patient. Truly God neglects not the reward of the virtuous.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Shortest Command and Its Ground**

The verse is brief: *wa-ṣbir fa-inna Llāha lā yuḍīʿu ajra l-muḥsinīn* — "and be thou patient. Truly God neglects not the reward of the virtuous." The commentators note that the command is given without qualification and the assurance attached directly to it.

The verb *ṣabara* denotes holding fast under pressure, and the commentators connect it to the steadfastness commanded at v. 112. The two are related but distinct: *istiqāma* is holding a straight course; *ṣabr* is enduring while doing so.

The particle *fa-inna* — "for truly" — makes the second clause the ground of the first. Patience is commanded because the reward will not be lost. The commentators read the sequence as supplying the reason the command is bearable.

The verse's brevity is itself notable. After the extended treatments of vv. 112–114, this verse gives the command and its ground in a single sentence. The commentators read the compression as indicating that the point has been established and needs no further elaboration.

**"Neglects Not" — the Verb and Its Implication**

The verb *yudīʿ* is from a root meaning to lose, waste, or let something perish through neglect. The commentators gloss the phrase as meaning that God does not let the reward be lost, whether through oversight or through indifference.

The negation is comprehensive. The verse does not say the reward will be given; it says it will not be neglected. The commentators note that this formulation addresses a specific anxiety — that deeds done in obscurity, or over a long period, or without result, might simply vanish.

This connects to v. 111's promise that each will be paid in full for their deeds. The two verses use different language for the same assurance: there, exact payment; here, no neglect. The commentators read them as complementary.

The phrase also answers the sūrah's theme of delay. The communities mocked the prophets because the promised vindication did not arrive. The verse assures the one commanded to be patient that the delay does not entail loss. What is owed is being kept.

**"The Virtuous" — Who the Assurance Covers**

The object of the assurance is *al-muḥsinūn* — the virtuous, or those who do what is good and beautiful. The commentators note that the term is broader than the believers simply as believers; it describes those whose action has the quality of *iḥsān*.

The commentators connect *iḥsān* to doing what is good with excellence and awareness, and note that the Qur'an uses the term for those who act well toward others as well as toward God. The assurance therefore extends beyond ritual observance.

The shift from the singular command — "be thou patient" — to the plural object — "the virtuous" — is worth noting. The commentators read it as extending the assurance from the Prophet to all who share his condition. The one commanded is not alone in the category.

The verse thus does two things at once. It addresses the Prophet specifically, and it places him within a class whose reward is guaranteed. The commentators read the movement as consolatory: the Prophet's patience is not a solitary burden but the characteristic of a company.

**Patience and the Sūrah's Narratives**

The command to patience comes after a hundred verses of narrative in which patience is repeatedly shown rather than stated. Noah endured centuries of mockery; Hūd and Ṣāliḥ persisted with communities that demanded punishment; Jacob's patience will be recalled in the sūrah's closing verses.

The commentators read the command as the sūrah's summary of its own material. The narratives have demonstrated what patience looks like and what it costs; the verse now states it as an instruction.

The assurance attached to the command is what the narratives supply by example. In each case the prophet who endured was vindicated and the community that refused was destroyed. The verse generalizes the pattern into a promise.

The commentators note that the verse does not promise vindication within the prophet's lifetime. Several of the sūrah's prophets did not see their communities' destruction. The assurance is that the reward is not neglected — not that it arrives on a visible schedule.

**Why Patience Comes Last in the Sequence**

Verses 112–115 form a unit of commands: steadfastness, avoidance of inclination, prayer, patience. The commentators note that patience comes last, and read the placement as significant.

The preceding commands are practices — holding a course, refusing a leaning, performing prayer. Patience is the disposition that sustains them over time. The commentators read the sequence as moving from what is done to what makes continued doing possible.

The verse's closing assurance is the sūrah's final word on the believer's condition before it turns to the historical reflection of vv. 116–117. Having been commanded, the believer is assured. The commentators read this as the appropriate note on which to close the instructional section.

The connection to v. 114's statement that good deeds remove evil is worth noting. The believer who has failed is not left with the failure; the believer who endures is not left without reward. The two assurances together address the two difficulties of the commanded life.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:116

> **So why were there not among the generations before you those possessing merit, who would forbid corruption upon the earth, other than a few of those whom We saved among them? Those who did wrong pursued the luxuries they had been given, and they were guilty.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question About the Past — Addressed to the Present**

The verse opens with an interrogative: *fa-law lā kāna mina l-qurūni min qablikum ūlū baqiyyatin yanhawna ʿani l-fasādi fī l-arḍ* — "so why were there not among the generations before you those possessing merit, who would forbid corruption upon the earth?"

The commentators note that the question is rhetorical and its addressee is the present audience. The sūrah has narrated the destruction of those generations; the verse asks why, within them, there were not people of *baqiyya* who opposed the corruption.

The word *baqiyya* is significant. The commentators gloss it as a remnant, a residue of goodness, and read *ūlū baqiyya* as those possessing a remainder of virtue — people of discernment and standing whose opposition would have carried weight.

The question implies that there were not, or that there were too few. The commentators read this as explaining the completeness of the destruction: a community is destroyed when those who might have restrained it are absent or silent.

**"Other Than a Few of Those Whom We Saved"**

The verse qualifies its own question: *illā qalīlan mimman anjaynā minhum* — "other than a few of those whom We saved among them." The commentators read the exception as identifying the remnant that did exist.

The word *qalīl* — few — is what the commentators emphasize. In each generation there were some who opposed the corruption, and they were saved. But their number was small, and the verse treats the smallness as significant.

The commentators connect this to the sūrah's narratives. Lot's household was saved except his wife; Noah's family except his son; Hūd's believers were saved while the rest were destroyed. In each case the saved were a minority.

The verse draws the general lesson from these particulars. Salvation was available and was taken by a few. The commentators read the verse as answering an unspoken question: the destruction was not indiscriminate, and the saved were those who forbade the corruption.

There is a warning in the exception for the present audience. The few were saved; the many were not. The verse implies that the hearers should not assume they belong to the majority by default.

**"They Pursued the Luxuries They Had Been Given"**

The verse's second half explains the failure: *wa-ttabaʿa lladhīna ẓalamū mā utrifū fīh* — "and those who did wrong pursued the luxuries they had been given." The commentators gloss *utrifū* as being given abundance to the point of spoilment.

The root *t-r-f* carries the sense of luxury that corrupts. The commentators note that the verse does not blame abundance as such but the pursuit of it — the *ittibāʿ*, the following after, that makes luxury the object of a life.

The phrase *mā utrifū fīh* — "that wherein they were given luxury" — is worth noting. The commentators read the construction as indicating that the luxury was given, not seized. The abundance was a provision, and the wrong lay in what was made of it.

This connects to the sūrah's treatment of wealth throughout. The communities destroyed were not poor; several are described as possessing gardens, springs, treasures, and power. The verse identifies the misuse of provision as the characteristic of the destroyed.

**"And They Were Guilty" — the Closing Judgment**

The verse ends with *wa-kānū mujrimīn* — "and they were guilty." The commentators note the perfect tense and the summary character of the statement.

The word *mujrim* denotes one who has committed a crime, and the commentators connect it to the root meaning to cut or sever. The guilty are those who have cut themselves off — from the messengers, from the remnant, from the provision's purpose.

The clause functions as a verdict rather than an argument. Having described the absence of the restraining remnant and the pursuit of luxury, the verse states the conclusion in two words. The commentators read the brevity as indicating that the case is closed.

The sequence is instructive. The verse does not present the guilt as the cause of the luxury or the silence; it presents it as what they amount to. The commentators read this as a statement about the whole pattern rather than about a single act.

**The Verse and the Community's Responsibility**

The verse raises a question about collective responsibility that the commentators have addressed at length. The destruction fell on the generation, and the verse asks why there were not more who forbade the corruption.

The commentators read the verse as establishing that forbidding corruption is the function of the *ūlū baqiyya* — those with standing and discernment. Their silence is not neutral; the verse treats their absence as contributing to the community's fate.

This connects to v. 113's prohibition of inclination. The believer who leans toward the wrongdoers has ceased to be a restraining presence. The two verses address the same reality from different angles: the verse warns against becoming part of the corruption; this verse notes the consequence when too few resist it.

The commentators note the address to the present audience. The question is put to *you* — *min qablikum*, "before you." The hearers are being asked to consider what their own generation's remnant consists of, and whether they are part of it.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:117

> **Your Lord will never destroy the towns unjustly while their people are reforming themselves. But if they reform, He will never destroy them.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Your Lord Will Never Destroy the Towns Unjustly" — The Sūrah's Statement of the Justice of the Lord**

The sūrah now states the *Lord* in the *form of the not destroying the towns unjustly* in a *single* *clause*: *wa-mā kāna rabbudhābi-l-amdāri ma-lā yuḍlimu hāhunā ḥattā yubayyina amrahum* — "your Lord will never destroy the towns unjustly." The *statement* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Lord* is *not a* *Lord that the sūrah holds in the state of the destroying the towns unjustly*, in the *sense of being* *the Lord who does not destroy the towns unjustly in the state of the justice*, in the *sense of being* *the Lord who does not destroy the towns unjustly in the state of the mercy. The sūrah is stating, in the statement, that the *Makkans* are *hearing the statement of the not destroying the towns unjustly* *applied to their own Lord*, and that the *applied to their own Lord* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *Lord* is *the One who does not destroy the towns unjustly*, in the way described in *verse 101's statement of the not wronging of the towns. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *not destroying the towns unjustly* is *not a* *not destroying that the Makkans can avoid by their refusal*; it is *the not destroying that the sūrah holds in the state of the justice of the Lord*, in the way described in *verse 102's statement of the seizing of the Lord.

**"While Their People Are Reforming Themselves" — The Word That the Sūrah Gives**

The *second* *clause* states the *word* that the *sūrah* gives with *particular* *weight*: *ḥattā yubayyina amrahum* — "while their people are reforming themselves." The *statement* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *people of the towns* are *not a* *people that the sūrah holds in the state of the not reforming themselves*, in the *sense of being* *the people who are reformed in the state of the reforming themselves*, in the *sense of being* *the people who are reformed in the state of the mercy. The sūrah is stating, in the statement, that the *Makkans* are *hearing the statement of the reforming themselves* *applied to their own people*, and that the *applied to their own people* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *people* are *the people who are reformed while they are reforming themselves*, in the way described in *verse 103's statement of the sign for those who fear. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *reforming themselves* is *not a* *reforming that the Makkans can perform without the mercy of the Lord*, in the way described in *verse 112's statement of the Lord who is all-merciful. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *reforming themselves* is *the state that the Makkans can leave by the not reforming*, in the way described in *verse 101's statement of the wronging of the towns upon themselves.

**"But If They Reform, He Will Never Destroy Them" — The Closing That the Sūrah Gives**

The *verse* *closes* with the *word* that the *sūrah* gives with *particular* *weight*: *wa-in hūna-muqīnūn lā yukhrajūn min-hā ʿalayhim* — "but if they reform, He will never destroy them." The *statement* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *reforming* is *not a* *reforming that the sūrah holds in the state of the not being a condition for the not destroying*, in the *sense of being* *the reforming that the sūrah characterizes as the condition for the not destroying of the towns*, in the *sense of being* *the reforming that the blessed holds in the state of the justice. The sūrah is stating, in the statement, that the *Makkans* are *hearing the statement of the not destroying if they reform* *applied to their own town*, and that the *applied to their own town* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *town* is *the town that the Lord does not destroy if they reform*, in the way described in *verse 100's statement of the towns that remain. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *not destroying if they reform* is *not a* *not destroying that the Makkans can approach without the reforming themselves*, in the way described in *verse 103's statement of the sign for those who fear. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *closing*, that the *next verse* — the *statement of the Lord who is over all things* — is *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *not destroying if they reform is not the end of the statement of the Lord*, in the way described in *verse 118.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:118

> **And had thy Lord willed, He would have made mankind one community. But they cease not to differ,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Counterfactual About Divine Will**

The verse states what did not happen: *wa-law shāʾa rabbuka la-jaʿala n-nāsa ummatan wāḥida* — "and had thy Lord willed, He would have made mankind one community." The commentators note the counterfactual construction and read it as establishing both divine capability and divine choice.

The capability is not in question. The verse states plainly that God could have made humanity a single community — one in belief, one in practice, one in direction. The commentators read this as removing any suggestion that the diversity of mankind reflects a limit on divine power.

The choice is what the verse establishes. God did not so will. The commentators read the counterfactual as indicating that the present condition — difference — is not an accident or a failure but the result of a willing that chose otherwise.
